## Page 1


ESSENTIALLY SINGULAR LIMIT OF JACOBI OPERATORS
AND APPLICATIONS TO HIGHER-ORDER SQUEEZING
FELIX FISCHER 1,*
, DANIEL BURGARTH 1
, AND DAVIDE LONIGRO 1
Abstract. We study a family of Jacobi operators in which the diagonal entries are multiplied by a
coupling parameter 𝜆≥0. Under suitable conditions, the operator is self-adjoint for every 𝜆> 0, while
the formal limit at 𝜆= 0 is a symmetric Jacobi operator admitting a one-parameter family of self-adjoint
extensions. A central ingredient of our analysis is the derivation of uniform bounds for square-summable
generalized eigenvectors in the small-𝜆regime, which combines discrete WKB methods with Airy-
function asymptotics. Using these estimates, we analyze the limiting behavior 𝜆→0 in the strong
resolvent sense, proving that for every sequence 𝜆𝑗→0 one can extract a subsequence along which
the corresponding Jacobi operators converge to some self-adjoint extension of the limiting operator;
conversely, every such extension can be obtained in this way. We call this behavior an essentially
singular limit, by analogy with essential singularities in complex analysis.
As an application, we study higher-order squeezing operators arising in quantum optics. Using the
connection with Jacobi operators, we show that when the relative strength of the free-field term tends
to zero, different self-adjoint extensions of the squeezing operator are selected along different sequences.
In particular, this limit does not single out a physically distinguished self-adjoint extension, but instead
identifies a distinguished subclass of extensions compatible with the underlying symmetry.
Keywords: Jacobi operators; Self-adjoint extensions; Strong resolvent convergence; Turning point analysis;
Higher-order squeezing.
1. Introduction
The relation between symmetric operators on Hilbert spaces and their self-adjoint extensions
is a central topic in operator theory and spectral analysis. A symmetric operator that fails to be
self-adjoint can admit a family of self-adjoint extensions with different spectral and dynamical
properties. Understanding how such extensions arise and whether they can be selected by natural
limiting procedures is therefore an important problem in operator theory. Questions of this type play
a prominent role in quantum mechanics, where self-adjointness is required for the generators of
dynamics, and different extensions may correspond to different boundary conditions or interactions.
Situations in which such questions arise naturally occur when studying limits of parameter-
dependent operators. A family of operators depending on a coupling parameter 𝜆may be self-adjoint
for all nonzero values of the parameter, while the formal limit obtained by removing the perturbation
yields a symmetric operator with nontrivial deficiency indices. The behavior of the resolvent in this
limit is therefore of particular interest, as it determines whether a specific self-adjoint extension is
selected or whether different extensions arise along different limiting sequences.
In this work we analyze this phenomenon for a class of Jacobi operators. Such operators play
an important role in spectral theory, appearing for instance in the study of orthogonal polynomials,
discrete Schrödinger operators, and representations of bosonic operators in Fock space. We consider
a family of Jacobi operators in which the diagonal entries are multiplied by a parameter 𝜆> 0,

ESSENTIALLY SINGULAR LIMITS OF JACOBI OPERATORS AND APPLICATIONS TO HIGHER-ORDER SQUEEZING

Under suitable assumptions, the resulting operator on the Hilbert space 𝓁2(ℕ), defined on the
subspace of compactly supported sequences, is essentially self-adjoint for every nonzero value of 𝜆,
whereas the formal limit obtained by setting 𝜆= 0 yields a symmetric Jacobi operator with deficiency
indices (1, 1), and therefore a one-parameter family of self-adjoint extensions.
Our main result (Theorem 2.7) concerns the behavior of this family in the limit 𝜆→0. We show
that the strong resolvent limit depends on the way in which the parameter tends to zero. More
precisely, for every sequence 𝜆𝑗→0 one can extract a subsequence along which the corresponding
operators converge in the strong resolvent sense to a self-adjoint extension of the limiting operator.
Conversely, every self-adjoint extension of the limiting operator arises as a strong resolvent limit
along a suitable sequence 𝜆𝑗→0. In this sense, this limit parametrizes the full family of self-adjoint
extensions. We refer to this phenomenon as an essentially singular limit, in analogy with the notion
of essential singularities in complex analysis.
A useful geometric picture of this phenomenon is shown in Fig. 1. For each 𝜆> 0, one can
associate to the Jacobi operator 𝒥(𝜆) a complex quantity encoding its boundary behavior at infinity.
As 𝜆→0, these values do not converge to a single distinguished limit. Instead, they move along
a closed curve associated with the limiting non-self-adjoint operator. Different sequences 𝜆𝑗→0
approach different points on this curve, and our main result shows that every point can arise in
this way. This picture is formalized later in terms of Weyl 𝑚-functions (Definition 3.12) and the
corresponding limit circle of the limiting Jacobi operator.

The result yields an explicit construction through which the full family of self-adjoint extensions of
a symmetric operator emerges in a small-coupling limit. This raises natural questions about whether
similar mechanisms exist for more general classes of operators. A key technical ingredient of our
analysis is the study of resolvent convergence in the limit. We first establish weak convergence of
generalized eigenvectors for suitable sequences of the coupling parameter. The main difficulty then
lies in upgrading this weak convergence to strong resolvent convergence; this step relies crucially on
the specific structure of Jacobi operators. The argument is highly nontrivial and does not appear to
extend directly beyond the Jacobi setting. One may expect that analogous phenomena could arise
for continuous models, such as Sturm–Liouville operators, where many of the underlying concepts


## Page 3


ESSENTIALLY SINGULAR LIMITS OF JACOBI OPERATORS AND APPLICATIONS TO HIGHER-ORDER SQUEEZING
3
admit direct counterparts. However, even for symmetric operators with deficiency indices (1, 1), it is
currently unclear whether an analogous result should hold in greater generality.
1.1. Previous results. Jacobi operators and their generalizations are ubiquitous in the literature,
appearing in areas such as solid-state physics [1–4], light–matter interaction [5–9], and the moment
problem [10–12], among many others [13–15]. They can be viewed as discrete counterparts of
Sturm–Liouville operators, and many results admit direct analogues in that setting; see, for instance,
[16–21]. General introductions to Jacobi operators can be found in [16, 22, 23], while non-symmetric
generalizations are discussed in [24–26].
Necessary and sufficient conditions for essential self-adjointness of Jacobi and block Jacobi op-
erators have been extensively studied; see, e.g., [27–41]. A large body of work is devoted to the
spectral and resolvent analysis of both bounded [42–47] and unbounded [35, 48–61] Jacobi operators.
Perturbation theory for essentially self-adjoint Jacobi operators is well understood; see, for example,
[62–72]. For the particular case of compact perturbations, see [32, 73–75]. However, none of the
works mentioned above considers a limiting operator that fails to be self-adjoint.
Similarly, the general theory of analytic perturbations (see, e.g., [76, 77] for an introduction) deals
with small perturbations of a self-adjoint operator. The situation is less well understood when the
limiting operator is not self-adjoint. In the semibounded case, perturbations typically converge to a
unique self-adjoint extension of the limiting operator; in this context, the Kato–Robinson theorem [77,
78] plays a central role. Self-adjoint extensions can also be parametrized via unbounded finite-rank
perturbations, which are usually treated within the framework of singular perturbation theory [79–81].
By contrast, in our setting the limiting operator is not semibounded, and the diagonal perturbation
has full (infinite) rank.
Related phenomena appear in the renormalization of singular potentials [82–84]; see also [85,
86] for the Aharonov–Bohm effect. In these cases, singularities at the origin lead to Schrödinger or
Dirac operators that are not self-adjoint. Self-adjointness can be restored by introducing a cutoff at a
finite radius, and the self-adjoint extensions of the original operator are then obtained by removing
the cutoff. Crucially, additional parameters of the regularization must be tuned in order to recover
different extensions.
The result obtained in this work is qualitatively reminiscent of Picard’s Great Theorem in complex
analysis [87, Theorem 11.3.2], a refinement of the Casorati–Weierstrass theorem. There, an analytic
function 𝑓(𝜆) with an essential singularity at 𝜆= 0 attains, in any neighborhood of the origin, all
complex values with at most one exception; in particular, for every 𝑧∈ℂthere exists a sequence
𝜆𝑗→0 such that 𝑓(𝜆𝑗) →𝑧. In a similar spirit, our main result shows that, as 𝜆→0, the family of
operators 𝐽(𝜆) approaches all self-adjoint extensions of the limiting operator along suitable sequences
of the parameter. In this sense, the limit 𝜆→0 behaves analogously to an essential singularity, in that
it allows one to approximate arbitrarily prescribed self-adjoint extensions along suitable sequences.
A key technical ingredient of our proof is the derivation of bounds on generalized eigenvectors of
the Jacobi operators under consideration. A variety of such bounds for self-adjoint operators can be
found in the literature on the spectral theory of Jacobi operators cited above; for works specifically
devoted to this topic, see [43, 88, 89]. Closely related is the study of asymptotics for linear recurrence
relations and difference equations. The classical theory, initiated by Birkhoff [90–92], was later
refined and systematically developed in a series of works by Wong and Wang [93–98]. Finally, of
particular relevance to the present work are results on discrete WKB methods and turning point
theory [99–101], which we adapt and extend to our setting.
1.2. Connection with higher-order squeezing. The original motivation for our study comes from
quantum optics. One of its central concepts is the squeezing of light, as introduced by Walls [102].
Under the dynamics generated by the second-order Hamiltonian
𝐴2 = (𝑎†)2 + 𝑎2 ,
(1.2)
coherent states evolve into squeezed states, in which the uncertainty of one field quadrature is
reduced at the expense of increased uncertainty in the conjugate one. On the experimental side, such


## Page 4


ESSENTIALLY SINGULAR LIMITS OF JACOBI OPERATORS AND APPLICATIONS TO HIGHER-ORDER SQUEEZING
4
squeezed states are routinely realized in optical parametric oscillators [103, 104] and superconducting
circuits [105, 106], and they constitute a key resource for surpassing the standard quantum limit. In
particular, they enable enhanced measurement precision in gravitational-wave detection [107–110],
as well as the generation of nonclassical states relevant for bosonic quantum error correction and
quantum information processing [111–119].
While conventional (quadrature) squeezing is by now a well-established and controllable resource,
there is growing interest in moving beyond Gaussian operations toward genuinely nonlinear, non-
Gaussian regimes [120–123]. In particular, non-Gaussian gates are necessary for universal bosonic
quantum computation [124]. A natural class of candidates for such interactions is given by
𝐴𝑘= (𝑎†)𝑘+ 𝑎𝑘,
(1.3)
with 𝑘≥3. These operators were introduced by Braunstein and McLachlan in the 1980s [125]
as higher-order generalizations of the squeezing interaction. Beyond their intrinsic conceptual
interest, these operators have been the subject of a longstanding debate concerning the existence and
uniqueness of the dynamics they generate; see, e.g., [9, 126–145]. In parallel, there has been recent
experimental progress in the realization of higher-order photon processes [146–153]. Of particular
relevance to the present work is the recent experimental realization of third- and fourth-order
squeezing reported in [153]. There, trapped ions subject to spin-dependent forces in a magnetic trap
are used to engineer effective higher-order bosonic interactions. By applying suitable pulse sequences,
the resulting dynamics are argued, via Floquet–Magnus techniques [154, 155], to approximate
Hamiltonians of the form (1.3). In this context, a rigorous operator-theoretic understanding of
higher-order squeezing Hamiltonians becomes essential.
In previous work [140, 143], we analyzed these operators, which are naturally defined on the
finite linear span of Fock states. Using the Birkhoff–Trjitzinsky theory of asymptotic expansions for
recurrence relations, we showed that they admit families of self-adjoint extensions whose dimension
depends on the order of squeezing. In particular, a choice of self-adjoint extension of 𝐴𝑘is required
in order to obtain a well-defined unitary dynamics. We further showed that finite-dimensional
truncations of the corresponding Fock space dynamics (i.e., restrictions to states with finitely many
photon excitations) reproduce the dynamics associated with two distinguished self-adjoint extensions,
depending on the truncation scheme. Moreover, adding a free-field term which is polynomial in the
number operator 𝑎†𝑎of degree ℎ> 𝑘, for example
𝐴𝑘,ℎ(𝐾) = (𝑎†)𝑘+ 𝐾(𝑎†𝑎)ℎ+ 𝑎𝑘,
(1.4)
with 𝐾> 0, yields an essentially self-adjoint operator for all values of 𝐾, thereby acting as a reg-
ularization mechanism. This naturally raises the question of whether a physically distinguished
self-adjoint realization of higher-order squeezing emerges in the regime where the strength of the
free-field term becomes small compared to the squeezing interaction, that is, in the limit 𝐾→0.
In the present work, we address this question by expressing higher-order squeezing operators as
direct sums of Jacobi operators. This connection not only allows us to revisit the parametrization of
their self-adjoint extensions, yielding a new and more direct proof that avoids the use of Birkhoff–
Trjitzinsky theory, but also enables a detailed analysis of the small-coupling limit 𝐾→0. We show
that, in this limit, different self-adjoint extensions of the squeezing operator are selected along
different sequences of the coupling parameter (Theorem 2.11). In particular, the limiting procedure
selects only those extensions that are compatible with the natural decomposition of the operator into
independent sectors. From a physical perspective, these are exactly the extensions that preserve the
2𝜋∕𝑘-rotational symmetry of the unperturbed operator 𝐴𝑘,ℎ(0). In this sense, the small-coupling limit
does not merely select a self-adjoint realization, but rather selects a symmetry-preserving subclass of
all possible extensions.
1.3. Outline. The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we introduce the notation used
throughout the paper and state our main results: Theorem 2.7, concerning the limiting behavior of
the family of Jacobi operators as 𝜆→0, and Theorem 2.11, which applies this result to higher-order
squeezing operators. In Section 3, we recall relevant concepts from the general theory of Jacobi


## Page 5

5
operators, establish spectral properties of the operator family under consideration, and prove the
main result, Theorem 2.7. The proof of Theorem 2.7 relies on a key technical input concerning the
asymptotics of generalized eigenvectors of Jacobi operators, Theorem 2.8. Since this constitutes the
most technically involved part of the analysis, it is developed independently in Section 4. Finally, in
Section 5, we prove Theorem 2.11. In Appendices A and B, we recall and prove several properties of
Airy functions and notions from turning point theory that are needed in the analysis of generalized
eigenvector asymptotics.

Note that, here and in the following, we use calligraphic letters such as 𝒥(𝜆) to denote the Jacobi
operator acting on the full space of sequences 𝓁(ℕ), i.e. defined purely by its formal action. By
contrast, straight letters such as 𝐽(𝜆) denote its realization as a (generally unbounded) operator on a
dense subspace of the Hilbert space 𝓁2(ℕ), obtained by restricting 𝒥(𝜆) to an appropriate domain.
Also note that the closure 𝐽(𝜆) of 𝐽min(𝜆) always exists as 𝐽min(𝜆) is symmetric.
We begin with the following observation:
Proposition 2.5 (cf. Propositions 3.20 and 3.26). 𝐽(𝜆) is self-adjoint if and only if 𝜆> 0. In particular,
𝐽(0) admits a one-parameter family of self-adjoint extensions (𝐽𝑡)𝑡∈ℝ.
Above and in the following, ℝ= ℝ∪{∞}. We refer to Proposition 3.10 for the explicit parametriza-
tion of all self-adjoint extensions 𝐽𝑡of 𝐽(0). Further properties of the operators 𝐽(𝜆) and 𝐽𝑡are
introduced and discussed in Section 3.
Remark 2.6. While our assumptions include 𝛼> 4∕3, Proposition 2.5 (in particular, the fact that 𝐽(0)
admits a one-parameter family of self-adjoint extensions) remains valid under the weaker condition
𝛼> 1. The stronger assumption 𝛼> 4∕3 is, however, essential for the proof of the main result,
Theorem 2.7; see Remark 3.35 and Section 4.7 for further discussion.
Therefore, 𝐽(𝜆) is self-adjoint for 𝜆> 0 and generates the unitary dynamics 𝑈𝜆(𝑡) = e−i𝐽(𝜆)𝑡,
whereas the limit operator 𝐽(0) admits multiple self-adjoint extensions. This raises the question
of the behavior of 𝐽(𝜆), its resolvents, and its dynamics as 𝜆→0. The limit is singular in several
respects: 𝐽(0) admits multiple self-adjoint extensions, their domains 𝒟(𝐽𝑡) strictly contain 𝒟(𝐽(𝜆))
(cf. Section 3), and the resulting operators 𝐽𝑡are unbounded from below.
These features prevent the direct application of standard perturbative techniques. In particular,
analytic perturbation theory does not apply due to the discontinuous change of domains (see, e.g., [76,
Chapter XII]), while the lack of semiboundedness excludes the use of the Kato–Robinson theorem
and related results (see, e.g., [78, Theorem 10.4.2]). The relation to other perturbative results is
discussed in Section 1.1. To the best of our knowledge, the limit 𝜆→0 therefore lies beyond the
reach of standard techniques.
We now present the main result of the paper:

Theorem 2.7. The following statements hold:
(i) Let (𝜆𝑗)𝑗∈ℕbe a sequence with 𝜆𝑗> 0 for all 𝑗∈ℕand 𝜆𝑗→0. Then there exist a subsequence

𝐽(𝜆𝑗) converges to 𝐽𝑡in the strong resolvent sense.
Moreover, in both cases the eigenvalues of the corresponding operators converge.

This result was already established in [140], where it was obtained via a detailed asymptotic analysis
based on the classical Birkhoff–Trjitzinsky theory. The approach developed there provides a complete
and precise description of the deficiency structure in this setting. In contrast, the Jacobi operator
point of view adopted in the present work leads to a more direct argument. Beyond its conceptual
simplicity, this formulation is also more flexible, especially in view of possible extensions to related
bosonic models, where similar ideas can be applied.

to 𝑡= ∞, cf. [143, Theorem 1].

3. Proof of Theorem 2.7
In this section we prove the main result of this work, Theorem 2.7. The argument relies crucially
on spectral properties of Jacobi operators, which we collect here. In addition to Proposition 2.5, we
will in particular use the following facts:
(a) The spectra of distinct self-adjoint extensions 𝐽𝑡of 𝐽(0) are discrete, mutually disjoint, and
together cover the whole real line (Proposition 3.22);
(b) The eigenvalues of the operators 𝐽(𝜆), 𝜆> 0, depend analytically on 𝜆(Corollary 3.32) and
diverge to −∞as 𝜆→0 (Proposition 3.41);
(c) The square-summable generalized eigenvectors of the operators 𝐽(𝜆) are uniformly bounded
in 𝜆by a square-summable sequence (Theorem 3.34).
The proof proceeds in two steps, corresponding to the two parts of Theorem 2.7. We first establish
Theorem 2.7(i). To this end, we show that for every sequence of positive real numbers (𝜆𝑗)𝑗∈ℕone
can extract a subsequence along which the generalized eigenvectors of 𝐽(𝜆𝑗) converge weakly to
those of a self-adjoint extension of 𝐽(0). The key step is then to upgrade this weak convergence to
strong resolvent convergence. Property (c) plays a crucial role in this step, which constitutes the most
technically demanding part of the argument.
We then prove Theorem 2.7(ii) using the following strategy:
(1) Given 𝑡∈ℝand 𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽𝑡), we construct a sequence (𝜆𝑗)𝑗∈ℕwith 𝜆𝑗> 0 and 𝜆𝑗→0 such
that 𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽(𝜆𝑗)) for all 𝑗∈ℕ;
(2) By Theorem 2.7(i), there exist a subsequence (𝜆𝑗𝑘)𝑘∈ℕand a parameter ̃𝑡∈ℝsuch that 𝐽(𝜆𝑗𝑘)
converges to 𝐽̃𝑡in the strong resolvent sense. Consequently, the spectra of 𝐽(𝜆𝑗𝑘) converge to
the spectrum of 𝐽̃𝑡, implying 𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽̃𝑡);
(3) Since the spectra of the self-adjoint extensions of 𝐽(0) are disjoint, this implies 𝑡= ̃𝑡, and the
subsequence (𝜆𝑗𝑘)𝑘∈ℕis the desired sequence.
The remainder of this section is organized as follows. In Section 3.1, we recall the necessary
properties of Jacobi operators. In Section 3.2, we analyze the spectral properties of the operators
𝐽(𝜆) and 𝐽𝑡. Finally, Section 3.3 contains the proof of Theorem 2.7 following the strategy outlined
above. The proof relies on a uniform bound on the square-summable generalized eigenvectors of
𝐽(𝜆) (Theorem 3.34), whose quite technical proof is postponed to Section 4.
3.1. Preliminaries. In this section, we collect notation and recall general properties of Jacobi
operators that will be used throughout the remainder of the analysis. At this stage, we do not restrict
to the specific class of operators introduced in Section 2.1, nor do we assume that Hypothesis 2.2
holds.

In the literature, solutions of Eq. (3.5) for some 𝑧∈ℂare commonly referred to as generalized

By the theory of linear recurrence relations, Eq. (3.5) admits a two-dimensional space of solutions
in 𝓁(ℕ). Equivalently, for any prescribed initial values 𝑢0, 𝑢1 ∈ℂ, there exists a unique solution
𝑢∈𝓁(ℕ) satisfying these initial conditions [160, Theorem 2.7]. We now introduce a pair of linearly
independent solutions of Eq. (3.5), obtained from specific choices of initial data, which play a central
role in the theory of Jacobi operators [16, Chapter 16]:

We return to the operator-theoretic properties of 𝑇and recall a fundamental criterion for the
essential self-adjointness of the operator 𝑇∶𝒟(𝑇) ⊂𝓁2(ℕ) →𝓁2(ℕ) associated with 𝒯; see, e.g., [10,
Proposition 6.5, Corollary 6.7, Theorem 6.16] (cf. also Proposition 3.7):
Proposition 3.5. Let 𝒯be a Jacobi operator, and 𝑇the corresponding operator on 𝓁2(ℕ) from Defini-
tion 3.1. Then 𝑇∗= 𝑇max, and the deficiency indices of 𝑇are either (0, 0) or (1, 1). In particular, 𝑇is
essentially self-adjoint if and only if 𝑃(𝑧) ∉𝓁2(ℕ) for some, and then all, 𝑧∈ℂ⧵ℝ, and is otherwise
not essentially self-adjoint.
In the context of Jacobi operators, the first case is called the limit point case, while the second is
referred to as the limit circle case; we refer to Remark 3.18 for an explanation of this terminology. A
well-known sufficient (but not necessary) condition for essential self-adjointness is the following
one, see [11, p. 24]:
Proposition 3.6 (Carleman condition). 𝑇is essentially self-adjoint whenever the following condition
holds:

The following result provides a characterization of the deficiency index dichotomy (Proposition 3.5)
in terms of square-summable solutions of the generalized eigenvalue equation, and is known as the
Weyl alternative [22, Lemmas 2.15–2.16]:
Proposition 3.7 (Weyl alternative). Let 𝒯be a Jacobi operator. Then exactly one of the following holds:
(i) For one (and hence all) 𝑧∈ℂ, all solutions of Eq. (3.5) are square-summable. In this case 𝒯is
limit circle, i.e. 𝑇is not essentially self-adjoint.
(ii) For one (and hence all) 𝑧∈ℂ, there exists, up to scalar multiples, a unique square-summable
solution of Eq. (3.5). In this case 𝒯is limit point, i.e. 𝑇is essentially self-adjoint.

Remark 3.16. If 𝒯is limit point, 𝑇is self-adjoint, and thus has no other maximally accretive or
dissipative extensions. Hence, 𝑢(𝑧) is the unique square-summable solution of Eq. (3.5) described in
Proposition 3.7. It is also sometimes called the Weyl solution [51].
The Weyl 𝑚-function 𝑚(𝑧) plays a crucial role in the spectral analysis of Jacobi operators. For self-
adjoint extensions 𝑇, 𝑚(𝑧) is a Nevanlinna (or Herglotz) function [16, Proposition 16.18] and coincides
with the Stieltjes transform of the spectral measure of 𝑇. Hence the entire spectral information of 𝑇
is encoded in 𝑚(𝑧).
In the non-essentially self-adjoint case, we denote by 𝑚(𝑧, 𝑡) the Weyl 𝑚-function of the maximally
accretive or dissipative extension 𝑇𝑡, and by 𝐺𝑛𝑚(𝑧, 𝑡) its Green function. The function 𝑚(𝑧, 𝑡) can be
expressed explicitly in terms of the orthogonal polynomials:

Remark 3.18. For any fixed 𝑧∈ℂwith Im 𝑧> 0, the values of 𝑚(𝑧, 𝑡) as 𝑡ranges over ℝlie on a circle
in ℂ+, which motivates the terminology “limit circle”. In contrast, in the essentially self-adjoint case
there is only one closed extension of 𝑇min, and hence only a single Weyl 𝑚-function. In this sense the
circle degenerates to a single point, which is consistent with the complementary terminology “limit
point”.

This concludes the analysis of 𝐽(0) and its extensions.
We now turn to the operators 𝐽(𝜆) for 𝜆> 0. Our strategy is to view the minimal operator
𝐽min(𝜆), defined on 𝓁0(ℕ) (cf. Definition 2.4), as a perturbation of 𝐽min(0) by decomposing it as
𝐽min(𝜆) = 𝐽min(0) + 𝜆𝐹, where 𝐹is the Jacobi operator determined by the diagonal entries of 𝐽min(𝜆).
We will exploit this decomposition to analyze the spectral properties of 𝐽(𝜆) in terms of those of 𝐹. The
rationale behind this approach lies in the scaling assumptions in Hypothesis 2.2: since 𝑎𝑛∼𝑛𝛼and
𝑓𝑛∼𝑛𝛽with 𝛽> 𝛼, the diagonal part grows asymptotically faster than the off-diagonal coefficients,
suggesting that 𝐽min(0) behaves as a lower-order perturbation of 𝐹as long as 𝜆> 0. This heuristic
will be made precise below.
Definition 3.23. 𝐹∶𝒟(𝐹) ⊂𝓁2(ℕ) →𝓁2(ℕ) is the unique closed operator satisfying 𝐹𝑒𝑛= 𝑓𝑛𝑒𝑛.
We gather here some properties of this operator:
Proposition 3.24. 𝐹is a nonnegative self-adjoint operator and has a core in 𝓁0(ℕ). Besides, its spectrum
𝜎(𝐹) satisfies
𝜎(𝐹) = {𝑓𝑛}𝑛∈ℕ
(3.56)
and is purely discrete, that is, its essential spectrum is empty: 𝜎ess(𝐹) = ∅.
Proof. By definition, (𝑒𝑛)𝑛∈ℕis a complete eigenbasis of 𝓁2(ℕ), the corresponding eigenvalues {𝑓𝑛}𝑛∈ℕ
being nonnegative. The only remaining statement to prove is 𝜎ess(𝐹) = ∅. By Hypothesis 2.2,
𝑓𝑛= 𝑛𝛽(1 + 𝑂(1∕𝑛)), and therefore lim𝑛→∞𝑓𝑛= ∞. Hence, the eigenvalues 𝑓𝑛diverge to +∞and
therefore have no finite accumulation point. Since (𝑒𝑛)𝑛∈ℕis a complete eigenbasis, it follows that
the spectrum is purely discrete and 𝜎ess(𝐹) = ∅.
□

We will hereafter denote by 𝑃𝜆(𝑧), 𝑄𝜆(𝑧) the orthogonal polynomials of first and second kind
associated with the Jacobi operator 𝐽(𝜆), see Definition 3.1.
Proposition 3.26. For every 𝜆> 0, 𝐽(𝜆) is an unbounded self-adjoint operator with domain 𝒟(𝐽(𝜆)) =
𝒟(𝐹), is bounded from below, and has purely discrete spectrum consisting of eigenvalues with multiplicity
one. Besides, given any eigenvalue 𝐸of 𝐽(𝜆), the corresponding eigenvector is given by the orthogonal
polynomial of first kind 𝑃𝜆(𝐸).
Proof. By Lemma 3.25, 𝐽(0) is infinitesimally relatively bounded with respect to 𝐹, and therefore
with respect to 𝜆𝐹for every 𝜆. As 𝜆𝐹is self-adjoint and nonnegative (Proposition 3.24), the Kato–
Rellich theorem [19, Theorem X.12] implies that 𝐽(𝜆) is self-adjoint on 𝒟(𝐽(𝜆)) = 𝒟(𝜆𝐹) = 𝒟(𝐹)
and bounded from below.
Furthermore, again by Lemma 3.25, 𝐽(0) is relatively compact with respect to 𝐹𝑘for some 𝑘∈ℕ,
and hence with respect to (𝜆𝐹)𝑘. Using Weyl’s essential spectrum theorem [76, Theorem XIII.14,
Corollary 3], this implies that the essential spectra of 𝐽(𝜆) and 𝜆𝐹are equal; by Proposition 3.24,
𝜎𝑒𝑠𝑠(𝐽(𝜆)) = 𝜎𝑒𝑠𝑠(𝜆𝐹) = 𝜎𝑒𝑠𝑠(𝐹) = ∅.
(3.72)

Remark 3.27. The fact that all eigenvalues have multiplicity one could also be proven with a more
abstract argument: 𝑒0 is a cyclic vector for 𝐽(𝜆) [16, p. 366], and hence the spectrum of 𝐽(𝜆) is simple,
cf. [16, Corollary 5.19].
Remark 3.28. The definition 𝐽(𝜆) = 𝐽(0) + 𝜆𝐹extends naturally to complex values of the parameter
𝜆. In this case, 𝜆𝐹is understood as a (generally non-symmetric) operator obtained by scalar multi-
plication of 𝐹by 𝜆∈ℂ. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition 3.26, and applying the Kato–Rellich
theorem to the perturbation 𝜆𝐹, one finds that for every 𝜆∈ℂ⧵{0} the operator 𝐽(𝜆) is closed and
satisfies 𝒟(𝐽(𝜆)) = 𝒟(𝐹). In particular, the family (𝐽(𝜆))𝜆∈ℂ⧵{0} is well-defined as a family of closed
operators with parameter-independent domain.
Having established that the spectrum of 𝐽(𝜆) is bounded from below and consists of simple eigen-
values, we proceed with determining their properties.
Definition 3.29. For 𝜆> 0, we denote by 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) the 𝑗th eigenvalue of 𝐽(𝜆), starting from the bottom
of the spectrum.
We begin by showing that the eigenvalues 𝐸𝑗(𝜆) are analytic in 𝜆and do not cross (Corollary 3.32),
and are monotonically decreasing as 𝜆→0 (Lemma 3.33). To prove these results, we will use analytic
perturbation theory; see, for instance, [76, Chapter XII.2] for an overview.
Definition 3.30. Let 𝑅⊂ℂbe a connected domain in the complex plane, and (𝑇(𝑟))𝑟∈𝑅a family of
closed operators with non-empty resolvent set. (𝑇(𝑟))𝑟∈𝑅is
(i) an analytic family in the sense of Kato if, for all 𝑟0 ∈𝑅, there exists 𝑧0 ∈𝜌(𝑇(𝑟0)) and a
neighborhood of 𝑟0 such that 𝑧0 ∈𝜌(𝑇(𝑟)) for all 𝑟near 𝑟0, and that 𝑟↦(𝑇(𝑟) −𝑧0)−1 is an
analytic operator-valued function of 𝑟near 𝑟0;
(ii) an analytic family of type (A) if 𝒟(𝑇(𝑟)) = 𝒟is independent of 𝑟∈𝑅, and if, for all 𝜓∈𝒟,
𝑟↦𝑇(𝑟)𝜓is an analytic vector-valued function of 𝑟.
If (𝑇(𝑟))𝑟∈𝑅is an analytic family of type (A), it is analytic in the sense of Kato [76, p. 16]. The follow-
ing lemma shows that (𝐽(𝜆))𝜆>0 can be embedded in such a family. To this end, note (cf. Remark 3.28)
that 𝐽(𝜆) admits a natural extension to complex nonzero values of 𝜆, and is a closed operator with
𝒟(𝐽(𝜆)) = 𝒟(𝐹).
Lemma 3.31. There exists an open set 𝑅⊃ℝ+ such that (𝐽(𝜆))𝜆∈𝑅is an analytic family of type A. In
particular, it is an analytic family in the sense of Kato.
Proof. Fix 𝜆0 > 0. For 𝜆in a (complex) neighborhood of 𝜆0 we can write
𝐽(𝜆) = 𝐽(𝜆0) + (𝜆−𝜆0)𝐹
on 𝒟(𝐹).

results (see, e.g., [76, Section XII.2]) that there exists a neighborhood 𝑂𝜆0 ⊂ℂof 𝜆0 such that

Corollary 3.32. The functions ℝ+ ∋𝜆↦𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) ∈ℝare real-analytic and do not cross, that is,
𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) = 𝐸(𝑘)(𝜆) for some 𝜆> 0 implies 𝑗= 𝑘. Moreover, the associated eigenvectors can be chosen to
depend analytically on 𝜆.
Proof. By Proposition 3.26, all eigenvalues 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) are simple for every 𝜆> 0. Since (𝐽(𝜆))𝜆∈𝑅is
an analytic family in the sense of Kato (Lemma 3.31), it follows from [76, Theorem XII.8] that the
eigenvalues and the corresponding spectral projections depend analytically on 𝜆. In particular, the
eigenvalues are real-analytic on ℝ+. Since the eigenvalues are simple for every 𝜆> 0, analytic
perturbation theory implies that the analytic eigenvalue branches cannot intersect; hence crossings
are absent. Finally, analyticity of the spectral projections implies that the corresponding eigenvectors
can be chosen to depend analytically on 𝜆.
□
Additionally, we obtain that the eigenvalues are monotonically decreasing as 𝜆→0:
Lemma 3.33. For all 𝜆> 0,
d𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆)
d𝜆
= ⟨𝜓𝑗(𝜆), 𝐹𝜓𝑗(𝜆)⟩≥0 ,
(3.76)
where 𝜓𝑗(𝜆) is the eigenvector associated with 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆).
Proof. By Lemma 3.31, both the eigenvalues 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) and the eigenvectors 𝜓𝑗(𝜆) are analytic in 𝜆.
Furthermore, for all 𝜆> 0, 𝒟(𝐽(𝜆)) = 𝒟(𝐹), and
d𝐽(𝜆)

= 𝐹
(3.77)
in the strong sense on 𝒟(𝐹). Hence, we can apply the Hellmann–Feynman theorem [165] to get the
equality in (3.76). As 𝐹is nonnegative, the claim follows.
□
Let us quickly summarize the findings of this section. For 𝜆= 0, the self-adjoint extensions
𝐽𝑡of the limit circle operator 𝐽min(0) have purely discrete spectra consisting of eigenvalues with
multiplicity one, which are distinct for different 𝑡, cf. Proposition 3.22. For 𝜆> 0, the spectrum of
each operator 𝐽(𝜆) is also purely discrete, and its eigenvalues 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) defined as in Definition 3.29
satisfy the following:
(i) 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) is of multiplicity 1, cf. Proposition 3.26;
(ii) 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) is analytic in 𝜆, and for different 𝑗, the 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) do not cross, cf. Corollary 3.32;
(iii) For all 𝑗∈ℕ, 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) is monotonically decreasing as 𝜆↓0, cf. Lemma 3.33.
In Proposition 3.41, we will additionally prove that for all 𝑗∈ℕlim𝜆→0 𝐸(𝑗)(𝜆) = −∞. These
properties will be essential in the proof of Theorem 2.7.

equation, Eq. (3.5), that are uniform in 𝜆≥0. For 𝒥(𝜆), Eq. (3.5) reads

upper half plane. As all 𝐽(𝜆𝑗) are self-adjoint, we also obtain
lim
𝑗→∞𝑀(𝑧∗, 𝜆𝑗) = lim
𝑗→∞𝑀(𝑧, 𝜆𝑗)∗= 𝑝∗,
(3.112)
and 𝑝∗= 𝑚(𝑧∗, 𝑡∗), cf. Lemma 3.17. Repeating the above analysis for 𝑧∗and 𝐽𝑡∗, we get

(3.113)
Hence, we have a sequence of resolvents of self-adjoint operators 𝐽(𝜆𝑗) converging strongly at both 𝑧
and 𝑧∗. Moreover, the limits (𝐽𝑡−𝑧)−1 and (𝐽𝑡∗−𝑧∗)−1 are resolvents of closed operators and therefore
have dense range. Thus, the assumptions of the Trotter–Kato theorem [163, Theorem VIII.22] are
satisfied. By the Trotter–Kato theorem [163, Theorem VIII.22], there exists a self-adjoint operator 𝐴
to which 𝐽(𝜆𝑗) converges in the strong resolvent sense. In particular,
(𝐽𝑡−𝑧)−1 = (𝐴−𝑧)−1,
(𝐽𝑡∗−𝑧∗)−1 = (𝐴−𝑧∗)−1,
(3.114)
implying 𝐽𝑡= 𝐽𝑡∗= 𝐴and thus completing the proof.
□
Remark 3.38. Theorem 3.34 is essential to establish strong resolvent convergence, since the self-
adjointness of the limiting operator 𝐽𝑡is not known a priori. Indeed, the pointwise convergence of the
Green functions 𝐺𝑛𝑚(𝑧, 𝜆𝑗) →𝑔𝑛𝑚(𝑧, 𝑡) yields convergence of the matrix elements of the resolvent,
i.e. weak resolvent convergence. If one knew in advance that 𝐽𝑡is self-adjoint (equivalently, that
𝑡= 𝑡∗), this would already suffice: for sequences of self-adjoint operators, weak and strong resolvent
convergence coincide (see [163, p. 284]).
However, in our setting the self-adjointness of 𝐽𝑡is itself part of the conclusion. Thus, weak resol-
vent convergence alone is not sufficient, and one cannot upgrade it to strong resolvent convergence
without additional input. This creates a bootstrapping problem: once strong resolvent convergence
is established, the limit must be self-adjoint, but proving strong resolvent convergence appears to
require this information beforehand. This issue is resolved by the explicit estimates provided in
Theorem 3.34, which allow us to prove strong resolvent convergence directly, without assuming
self-adjointness of the limit.

and 𝑡∈ℝsuch that 𝐽(𝜆𝑗𝑘) converges to the self-adjoint operator 𝐽𝑡in the strong resolvent sense.

(𝑀(𝑧, 𝜆𝑗))𝑗∈ℕis compact. Thus, there exists a convergent subsequence (𝑀(𝑧, 𝜆𝑗𝑘))𝑘∈ℕ. The statement
follows by applying Proposition 3.37 to the sequence (𝜆𝑗𝑘)𝑘∈ℕ.
□
Additionally, we show that, if 𝐽(𝜆𝑗) →𝐽𝑡in the strong resolvent sense, a real number is in the
spectrum of 𝐽𝑡if and only if it is the limit of a sequence of eigenvalues of the approximating operators.

as a direct consequence of Theorem 3.34.

Proof of Theorem 2.7. The first statement is Corollary 3.39.
For the second statement, let 𝑡∈ℝand 𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽𝑡). For 𝑙∈ℕand 𝜆> 0, let 𝐸(𝑙)(𝜆) be the 𝑙th lowest

𝜆> 0 and
lim
𝜆→0 𝐸(𝑙)(𝜆) = −∞.
(3.137)
Moreover, for fixed 𝜆> 0, 𝐽(𝜆) is unbounded from above and hence there exists 𝑙∈ℕsuch that
𝐸(𝑙)(𝜆) is arbitrarily big. We proceed to construct a sequence (𝜆𝑗)𝑗∈ℕwith lim𝑗→∞𝜆𝑗= 0 such that
𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽(𝜆𝑗)) for all 𝑗∈ℕ: To begin with, there exists 𝑙0 ∈ℕsuch that 𝐸(𝑙0)(1) > 𝐸, and by Eq. (3.137)
there exists 0 < 𝜆0 < 1 such that 𝐸(𝑙0)(𝜆0) = 𝐸. Next, there exists 𝑙1 ∈ℕsuch that 𝐸(𝑙1)(𝜆0∕2) > 𝐸,
and consequently there also exists 0 < 𝜆1 < 𝜆0∕2 such that 𝐸(𝑙1)(𝜆1) = 𝐸. Iterating this process we
construct a sequence (𝜆𝑗)𝑗∈ℕwith 0 < 𝜆𝑗≤2−𝑗and 𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽(𝜆𝑗)) for all 𝑗∈ℕ.
By Theorem 2.7(i), there exists a subsequence (𝜆𝑗𝑘)𝑘∈ℕand ̃𝑡∈ℝsuch that 𝐽(𝜆𝑗𝑘) converges to
𝐽̃𝑡in the strong resolvent sense. In particular, 𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽(𝜆𝑗𝑘)) for all 𝑘∈ℕ, and by Proposition 3.40
𝐸∈𝜎(𝐽̃𝑡). By Proposition 3.22, eigenvalues of different self-adjoint extensions are distinct, whence
̃𝑡= 𝑡.
□

Weyl 𝑚-functions 𝑀(𝑧, 𝜆) uniquely represent the operator 𝐽(𝜆). As 𝜆→0, the values of 𝑀(𝑧, 𝜆) for

4. Asymptotics of generalized eigenvectors
This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem 3.34 (cf. Theorem 2.8), which we utilize in the proof
of Proposition 3.37 to show that weak convergence of solutions of Eq. (3.78) as 𝜆→0 implies strong
resolvent convergence. This entails proving the following bound for square-summable solutions 𝑢𝜆,𝑧
of Eq. (3.78), with 𝑧ranging in some compact set Ω:

4.1. Heuristics and structure of the proof. We begin by providing an overview of the proof,
focusing on the most technically demanding points. Recall the generalized eigenvalue equation for
𝒥(𝜆), cf. Eq. (3.5):
𝑎𝑛𝑢𝑛+1 + (𝜆𝑓𝑛−𝑧)𝑢𝑛+ 𝑎𝑛−1𝑢𝑛−1 = 0

For large enough 𝑛, we get
𝑓𝑛

∼1, and

𝑎𝑛∼0. Hence, the above
equation is formally approximated by the following equation:
𝑢𝑛+1 + 𝜆𝑛𝛿𝑢𝑛+ 𝑢𝑛−1 = 0 .
(4.4)
It will be useful to recast this equation in terms of a discrete variable 𝑥. The relevant scale of this
problem is given by 𝑥= 𝑛ℎ, where ℎ= 𝜆1∕𝛿. Setting 𝑢𝑛= (−1)𝑛𝜓(𝑛ℎ) = (−1)𝑛𝜓(𝑥) for some
function 𝜓, Eq. (4.4) becomes
𝜓(𝑥+ ℎ) −𝑥𝛿𝜓(𝑥) + 𝜓(𝑥−ℎ) = 0
(4.5)
for all 𝑥= 𝑛ℎand 𝑛≥1. Recalling the definition of the discrete Laplacian, we can identify Eq. (4.5)
as the discrete analogue of the following differential equation:
∆𝜓(𝑥) = (𝑥𝛿−2)𝜓(𝑥) .
(4.6)
This is a second-order differential equation with a so-called turning point at 𝑥𝛿= 2. In the region
𝑥𝛿< 2, all solutions of Eq. (4.6) are oscillating and hence bounded. Instead, in the region 𝑥𝛿> 2, the
so-called recessive solution of Eq. (4.6) decays exponentially, whereas all other solutions are dominant
and grow exponentially. This is indeed reminiscent of the behavior of solutions of Eq. (4.2): from
the Weyl alternative theorem (Proposition 3.7) we know that one solution of Eq. (4.2), namely 𝑢𝜆,𝑧is
square-summable, whereas all others are not.
For 𝛿= 1, Eq. (4.6) is equivalent to the Airy equation, cf. Appendix A, and admits two linearly
independent solutions given by Ai(𝑥+ 2) and Bi(𝑥+ 2), where Ai and Bi are the Airy functions of
first and second kind [156, p. 392]. For 𝛿≠1, solutions of Eq. (4.6) can be approximated by Airy
functions via a suitable variable transformation 𝑥→𝜉(𝑥) corresponding to the so-called Langer
transform. Standard references on rigorous error analysis for such equations are [156, Chapter 11]
and [166, Chapter VIII].
We expect analogous considerations to apply to the discrete case (4.5). Fewer results are available
in this setting. An explicit solution for 𝛿= 1 is given in [167]. Wang and Wong [96, 97] study the
asymptotics of solutions of difference equations with a turning point; however, their work does not
provide bounds on these solutions near the turning point. Geronimo et al. [101, 168] and Fedotov
and Klopp [99] transfer results from the continuous case (with 𝑥ranging in some compact set 𝐾) to
the discrete case while keeping track of errors.

We will employ and adapt these results to our purposes. As we expect solutions of Eq. (4.5) to
exhibit a turning point at 𝑥𝛿= 2, our strategy is to divide the analysis into three overlapping regions
[0, 𝑥2], [𝑥1, 𝑥4], [𝑥3, ∞) (see Fig. 4), with 𝑥𝛿
2 < 2 < 𝑥𝛿
4, and diversify our bounding technique as
follows:
(i) For 𝑥∈[0, 𝑥2], we employ standard methods for difference equations, involving the use of
Turán determinants (cf. [40]), to bound all solutions of Eq. (4.5);
(ii) For 𝑥∈[𝑥1, 𝑥4], we adapt the turning-point analysis by Geronimo et al. and Fedotov–Klopp
to our case;
(iii) For 𝑥∈[𝑥3, ∞), we again use standard methods from the theory of difference equations,
namely Poincaré’s theorem, to obtain explicit asymptotics of the decaying solution, cf. [160,
Theorem 8.35].
The overlap between the three regions will allow us to connect the bounds obtained in each regime.
We carry out the analysis both in terms of the rescaled variable 𝑥and the original integer variable
𝑛. In particular, the boundary points 𝑥𝑗correspond to suitable 𝜆-dependent integers 𝑁𝑗(𝜆). The
strategy of the proof is organized as follows:
∙In Section 4.2, we define the boundary points 𝑁𝑗(𝜆) rigorously and establish the regularity
properties required for the analysis, using the asymptotics of the coefficients 𝑎𝑛and 𝑓𝑛. We
also show that the regime of small 𝑛, where the asymptotic expansions do not accurately
describe the coefficients, can be absorbed into a constant prefactor in the final bound; see
Lemma 4.5.
∙The oscillatory region 𝑛≤𝑁2(𝜆) is treated in Section 4.3 using Turán determinants. This
yields a uniform bound for all solutions of Eq. (4.2); see Proposition 4.10.
∙In Section 4.4, we analyze the intermediate region 𝑁1(𝜆) ≤𝑛≤𝑁4(𝜆) by constructing
approximate solutions of Eq. (4.2) in terms of Airy functions. Explicit error bounds are derived
in Proposition 4.21. These estimates, together with the properties of the Airy functions, are
then used in Proposition 4.23 to obtain bounds for the actual solutions.

∙Finally, in Section 4.5, we derive asymptotics for the recessive solutions of Eq. (4.2) using
Poincaré’s theorem, and obtain explicit error bounds for 𝑛≥𝑁4(𝜆); see Proposition 4.29.
In Section 4.6, all of these ingredients are combined to prove the final bound (4.1).

4.4. Second region: turning point analysis. We now turn to the second region 𝑛∈𝐾𝑁(𝜆) =
[𝑁1(𝜆), 𝑁4(𝜆)], corresponding to 𝑥∈𝐾𝑥= [𝑥1, 𝑥4]. Recalling Eq. (4.6), we expect Eq. (4.2) to admit
one solution that decays for 𝑥𝛿> 2, the so-called recessive solution 𝑢r,ℎ,𝑧, and one linearly independent
solution that grows for 𝑥𝛿> 2, the so-called dominant solution 𝑢d,ℎ,𝑧. For 𝑥𝛿< 2, both solutions
are expected to oscillate and remain bounded. Throughout this section, objects associated with the
recessive and dominant solutions will be labeled by the indices r and d, respectively.
Recalling the discussion in Section 4.1, we aim to approximate both the recessive and dominant
solutions with solutions of the Airy equation. An overview of relevant properties needed in this work
are discussed in Appendix A. To this end, we apply results of Geronimo et al. [168] to obtain explicit
error bounds on the approximate solutions. Our strategy for the analysis in the turning point region
is organized as follows.
(1) We begin by multiplying Eq. (4.2) by ℎ𝛼, which leads to a rescaled difference operator 𝐿ℎ,𝑧with
coefficient functions 𝑎1(𝑥, ℎ) and 𝑏1(𝑥, ℎ) (Lemma 4.11). By construction, these coefficients
are bounded on 𝐾𝑥× [0, ℎ], allowing us to apply the results of Geronimo et al.
(2) Next, we replace 𝑎1(𝑥, ℎ) and 𝑏1(𝑥, ℎ) with simplified coefficients ̃𝑎(𝑥, ℎ) and ̃𝑏(𝑥, ℎ), obtained
from the asymptotics of 𝑎𝑛and 𝑓𝑛. The assumptions in Hypothesis 2.2 imply that the original
operator 𝐿ℎ,𝑧(Lemma 4.12) can be treated as a perturbation of the simpler operator ̃𝐿ℎ
(cf. Definition 4.13 and Lemma 4.14). In particular, the contribution of the parameter 𝑧will
also be perturbative in the relevant regime.
(3) Using the coefficients ̃𝑎(𝑥, ℎ) and ̃𝑏(𝑥, ℎ), we then construct the Langer transform (Defini-
tion 4.17),
𝑥↦𝜉(𝑥, ℎ),
(4.63)
which maps the turning point of the difference equation to 𝜉(𝑥, ℎ) = 0. The transformed
equation is oscillatory for 𝜉(𝑥, ℎ) < 0, while for 𝜉(𝑥, ℎ) > 0 it admits a recessive and a
dominant solution. This matches the qualitative behavior of the Airy equation for negative
and positive arguments, respectively.
(4) Motivated by this correspondence, we construct approximate solutions (cf. Definition 4.20)
𝜓r(𝑥, ℎ),
𝜓d(𝑥, ℎ),
(4.64)
in terms of Airy functions. These functions solve the approximate equation
(̃𝐿ℎ𝜓)(𝑥) = 0
(4.65)
up to an error of order 𝑂(ℎ2) on 𝐾𝑥.

. Heuristically, the functions 𝑤r(𝑥, ℎ) and 𝑤d(𝑥, ℎ) may be viewed as envelopes
of the corresponding approximate solutions.
(6) The main estimate of Proposition 4.21 shows that the scaled errors satisfy bounds of order
ℎ1∕3. Consequently, the approximations improve as
ℎ= 𝜆1∕𝛿→0.
(4.68)
This allows us to derive quantitative bounds on the actual solutions 𝑢r,ℎ,𝑧and 𝑢d,ℎ,𝑧in Propo-
sition 4.23.
Throughout these steps, the precise value of Λ > 0 may vary and is implicitly allowed to decrease
from one statement to the next, cf. Remark 4.3.
For the convenience of the reader, Table 1 collects the main symbols used throughout this section.
A summary of the results of Geronimo et al. relevant to the present setting is given in Appendix B.
Related results in a similar setting were obtained by Fedotov and Klopp in [99].

convert the generalized eigenvalue equation (4.2) in a form compatible with the setting of [168],
cf. Eq. (B.1):

We now introduce a variant of the Langer transform, which plays a central role in the asymptotic
analysis of recurrence relations, cf. [99, 168]. Although the terminology originates from the differential
equation literature, where the Langer transform is more commonly used and takes a slightly different
form, we adopt here the convention standard in the literature on recurrence relations. We again refer
to Appendix B for the related results needed in the remainder of this section.
To this end, we first introduce an auxiliary function, cf. Eq. (B.4):

Proof. By Lemma 4.15, conditions (i)–(iii) are fulfilled. By construction, ̃𝑎(𝑥, ℎ) > 0 and ̃𝑏(𝑥, ℎ) ∈ℝ.

and 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) > 1 for 𝑥> 𝑥0(ℎ).
□

We are now ready to prove Theorem 3.34. The argument is a matching procedure across the three
regimes defined in Definition 4.1:
∙We first analyze the tail region 𝑛≥𝑁3(𝜆), where Lemmas 4.24 and 4.25 imply that any
square-summable solution coincides with the recessive one, and the Riccati formulation
yields uniform decay estimates.
∙We then propagate this information into the turning-point region 𝑁1(𝜆) ≤𝑛≤𝑁4(𝜆), where
Proposition 4.21 and Lemma 4.30 allow us to express the solution in terms of recessive and
dominant components and to control the contribution of the latter in the overlap.
∙Finally, we extend the resulting bounds to the interior region 𝑛≤𝑁0 using standard discrete
comparison estimates. Combining the three regimes yields a uniform polynomial bound for
all 𝑛∈ℕ, completing the proof.

4.7. Discussion of the bound. We briefly comment on the tightness of the bound provided by
Theorem 3.34. At first sight, the additional loss of 1∕6 in the exponent may appear surprising, since
in the unperturbed case 𝜆= 0 one obtains strictly sharper decay estimates for solutions of Eq. (4.2).
More precisely, while Theorem 3.34 yields a bound with an additional factor 𝑛1∕6 which is uniform
in 𝜆> 0, no such loss occurs for 𝜆= 0. The following proposition shows that in the uncoupled case
the optimal exponent 𝛼∕2 is recovered exactly. This is a direct consequence of [40, Theorem 2]; for
the convenience of the reader, we nevertheless include a short proof.

As shown in Lemma 3.36, solutions of Eq. (4.2) with 𝜆> 0 converge pointwise to solutions of the
limiting equation corresponding to 𝜆= 0 as 𝜆→0. At first sight, one might therefore expect that the
uniform bound from Theorem 3.34 should also exhibit the same decay rate 𝑛−𝛼∕2 as in the uncoupled
case, and that the additional factor 𝑛1∕6 is merely an artifact of the proof technique. However, the
additional factor arises precisely in the turning point region in the proof of Theorem 3.34. In particular,
by Proposition 4.10, all solutions of Eq. (4.2) are bounded by 𝐶𝑛−𝛼∕2 for all 𝑛≤𝑁2(𝜆) and 𝜆≥0. As
𝜆→0, 𝑁2(𝜆) diverges to ∞, cf. Definition 4.1, so that the turning point region disappears in the limit
circle case.
The following numerical analysis indicates that the additional factor of 1∕6 is strictly necessary in
the turning point region. More precisely, the solution develops a pronounced transition region near

sum of Jacobi operators defined by sequences satisfying Hypothesis 2.2.

We finally present the proof of the main result for higher-order squeezing operators, Theorem 2.11.
The proof is based on combining the Jacobi decomposition from Proposition 2.9 with the general
convergence theory developed for Jacobi operators in Theorem 2.7. The key point is that each invariant
subspace yields a family of Jacobi operators depending on the coupling parameter 𝜆: the global limit
is obtained by constructing compatible subsequences across all blocks and then assembling the
resulting strong resolvent limits via the direct-sum decomposition.

Appendix A. Airy and related functions
In this appendix, we recall basic facts about the Airy functions and related special functions. For a
comprehensive overview, see [156], in particular Sections 8.8, 11.1, and 11.8.
The Airy differential equation is given by [156, p. 392]
d2𝑦(𝑥)

(A.1)
A distinguished real solution is the Airy function of the first kind Ai(𝑥), which admits the integral
representation
Ai(𝑥) = 1

(A.2)
Its characteristic property is that Ai(𝑥) →0 as 𝑥→∞, and it is therefore referred to as the recessive
solution in this regime. A second linearly independent solution is the Airy function of the second
kind Bi(𝑥), which satisfies Bi(𝑥) →∞as 𝑥→∞and is correspondingly called the dominant solution.
Both Ai(𝑥) and Bi(𝑥) are oscillatory for 𝑥< 0.
In this work, we also require solutions of Eq. (A.1) on the complex plane. A convenient family of
three linearly independent solutions is given by
Ai𝑗(𝑥) = Ai(𝜔𝑗𝑥) ,

Here, ℤ3 denotes the cyclic group of integers modulo 3, which we identify with the set {0, 1, 2}
equipped with addition modulo 3. In particular, we freely identify indices modulo 3, so that Ai𝑗+1
and Ai𝑗−1 are well-defined for all 𝑗∈ℤ3 (e.g. Ai2+1 = Ai0). For convenience of notation, we also
occasionally use the equivalent labeling 𝑗∈{0, ±1}, where the identification is understood modulo 3
(for instance, −1 ≡2 mod 3). This alternative labeling is often more transparent when discussing
the Stokes sectors below.
The corresponding closed sectors 𝑆𝑗⊂ℂ, 𝑗∈ℤ3, are defined by [156, Section 11.8]

Of particular importance are the boundaries of the sectors 𝑆𝑗, given by 𝜔𝑗±1ℝ−. Along these
rays, the asymptotic behavior of Ai𝑗changes discontinuously in the sense described above. This is a
manifestation of the Stokes phenomenon, and the rays 𝜔𝑗ℝ−are therefore called the Stokes lines of
the Airy equation.

Appendix B. Discrete turning point theory
In this appendix, we summarize the discrete WKB and turning point theory developed by Geronimo,
Bruno, and Van Assche [168]; see also the related work of Fedotov and Klopp [99]. Since throughout
this work we only require the case 𝑦= 0 in the notation of [168], we suppress the dependence on the
spectral parameter 𝑦for simplicity.
To distinguish the notation in this appendix from that used in the main text, we employ capital
letters and consider two continuous coefficient functions 𝐴1, 𝐵1 ∈𝐶([𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻]) , where 0 ≤
𝑥𝑖< 𝑥𝑒< ∞and 𝐻> 0. We assume 𝐴1(𝑥, ℎ) > 0 for all (𝑥, ℎ) ∈[𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻], while 𝐵1(𝑥, ℎ) may
be complex-valued.
Given 𝑈= (𝑈(𝑛))𝑛∈ℕ∈𝓁(ℕ), we consider the second-order difference equation
𝐴1(𝑛ℎ, ℎ)𝑈(𝑛+ 1) + 𝐵1(𝑛ℎ, ℎ)𝑈(𝑛) + 𝐴1((𝑛−1)ℎ, ℎ)𝑈(𝑛−1) = 0
(B.1)
for all 0 < ℎ≤𝐻and 𝑛∈ℕsatisfying 𝑥𝑖≤𝑛ℎ≤𝑥𝑒. Compared with [168, Eq. 3.37], we shift
the argument in the second off-diagonal coefficient by −ℎin order to match the Jacobi operator
convention used in Eq. (3.5).
Our goal is to construct two linearly independent solutions 𝑈(1), 𝑈(2) of Eq. (B.1) and approximate
them in terms of Airy functions near a turning point. Following [168], we regard Eq. (B.1) as a
perturbation of a reference equation with coefficients 𝐴(𝑥, ℎ) and 𝐵(𝑥, ℎ). The deviation between
the two systems plays a central role in the error analysis, and we therefore introduce

for 0 < ℎ≤𝐻and 𝑛ℎ∈[𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒].
Following [168, Eqs. 2.7–2.8], we next introduce the associated Langer transform. For convenience,
we define
𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) = −
𝐵(𝑥, ℎ)
2𝐴(𝑥−ℎ∕2, ℎ) .
(B.4)
This quantity plays the role of 𝑣(𝑧)∕2 in the notation of [99].
We make the following assumptions:
Hypothesis B.1. Let 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) be given as in Eq. (B.4). Suppose that there exists an open region 𝑂⊂ℂ
with [𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] ⊂𝑂such that the following holds:
(i) For fixed ℎ∈[0, 𝐻], the function 𝑥↦𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) admits an analytic extension in 𝑥∈𝑂;
(ii) For every ℎ∈[0, 𝐻], there exists a unique solution 𝑥0(ℎ) such that 𝑞(𝑥0(ℎ), ℎ) = 1;
(iii) The turning point is simple, i.e. 𝑞′(𝑥0(ℎ), ℎ) ≠0 for all ℎ∈[0, 𝐻].
Moreover, for 𝑥∈ℝand ℎ∈[0, 𝐻], we additionally assume:
(iv) 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) ∈𝐶∞([𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻]);
(v) 𝐴(𝑥, ℎ) > 0 and 𝐵(𝑥, ℎ) ∈ℝ;
(vi) −1 < 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) < 1 for 𝑥< 𝑥0(ℎ), while 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) > 1 for 𝑥> 𝑥0(ℎ).
These conditions guarantee the existence of a simple turning point at 𝑥= 𝑥0(ℎ), separating an
oscillatory regime from an exponentially decaying or growing one. Under these assumptions, one
can introduce the associated Langer transform.

We come back to the construction of two linearly independent solutions 𝑈(1) and 𝑈(2) of the
difference equation (B.1) by means of Airy-type approximations near the turning point 𝑥0(ℎ). The
Langer transform 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ) introduced above plays the role of a canonical local coordinate in which
the difference equation asymptotically reduces to the Airy equation. Accordingly, the solutions 𝑈(1)
and 𝑈(2) will be approximated by Airy functions evaluated at ℎ−2∕3𝜌(𝑥, ℎ).

However, the existence of an approximate solution Ψ(𝑥, ℎ) satisfying Eq. (B.39) does not by itself
imply the existence of an exact solution of the corresponding difference equation which remains close
to Ψ(𝑥, ℎ). A fortiori, it does not imply that the original difference equation (B.1) with coefficients
𝐴1, 𝐵1 admits such a solution. Establishing this requires a detailed error analysis. Such an analysis
was carried out independently by Geronimo, Bruno and Van Assche [168] and by Fedotov and
Klopp [99]. Here we summarize the approach of Geronimo et al. [168].
To this end, we introduce two distinguished approximate solutions constructed from the Airy
functions Ai𝑗defined in Eq. (A.3), cf. [168, Eqs. 4.8–4.9]:

Remark B.9. Under additional assumptions, [168, Theorem 4.4] yields considerably simpler error
bounds. While the independence of the constants 𝑐1, 𝑐2, and 𝐶from the perturbative coefficients
𝐴1, 𝐵1 is not stated explicitly in [168, Theorem 4.4], it follows from the proof. In particular, the
argument provides explicit procedures for estimating these constants from above.
Geronimo et al.’s framework thus provides a method for constructing approximate solutions of
Eq. (B.1) in terms of Airy functions and the Langer transform. To apply these results, one must verify
Hypothesis B.1 and B.5, together with the remaining assumptions of Theorem B.8. The following
lemma shows that many of these properties already follow from Hypothesis B.1.
Lemma B.10. Assume that Hypothesis B.1 holds with open region 𝑂⊂ℂ. Then the following statements
hold for all (𝑥, ℎ) ∈[𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻]:
(i) 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ) ∈ℝ. In particular, 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ) ∈𝑆0 ⊂𝑆0 ∪𝑆1, where the sectors 𝑆𝑗are defined in Eq. (A.4);
(ii) 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ) < 0 for 𝑥< 𝑥0(ℎ), and 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ) > 0 for 𝑥> 𝑥0(ℎ);
(iii) Re 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ)3∕2 is strictly increasing in 𝑥;
(iv) 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ) ∈𝐶∞([𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻]). Moreover, for fixed ℎ∈[0, 𝐻], the map 𝑥↦𝜌(𝑥, ℎ) is analytic
in 𝑂;
(v) 𝜌(𝑥, ℎ)∕(𝑥−𝑥0(ℎ)) ∈𝐶2([𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻]) and is uniformly bounded away from 0;
(vi) 𝑔(𝑥, ℎ) ∈𝐶∞([𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻]), and for fixed ℎ∈[0, 𝐻], 𝑥↦𝑔(𝑥, ℎ) is analytic in 𝑂;
(vii) 𝐴(𝑥−ℎ∕2, ℎ)1∕2𝑔(𝑥, ℎ) is uniformly bounded above and below away from 0 on [𝑥𝑖, 𝑥𝑒] × [0, 𝐻].
In particular, Hypothesis B.5 is satisfied.
Proof. By assumption, 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) ∈[−1, 1] for 𝑥≤𝑥0(ℎ), hence arccos(𝑞(𝑥, ℎ)) ∈ℝ≥0 in this region.
Similarly, 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) ≥1 for 𝑥≥𝑥0(ℎ), so arccosh(𝑞(𝑥, ℎ)) ∈ℝ≥0. Statements (i)–(iii) follow directly
from Definition B.2 and Eq. (A.4).
We prove (iv). The function 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) is 𝐶∞in (𝑥, ℎ) and analytic in 𝑥for fixed ℎ. Moreover, for
each ℎ, the equation 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ) = 1 has a unique solution 𝑥0(ℎ) with 𝑞′(𝑥0(ℎ), ℎ) ≠0. By the implicit
function theorem, 𝑥0(ℎ) is locally 𝐶∞in ℎ; global 𝐶∞-regularity on [0, 𝐻] follows from uniqueness
and compactness.
By Lemma B.3,

To apply the results of Geronimo et al., it is therefore sufficient to verify the assumptions on 𝑞(𝑥, ℎ)
